  - D39 decision: applied mechanically, no other candidate is within 2 points,
    so the winner is llm with qwen. D40 is in docs/DECISIONS.md with a
    change-log line, and the LLM_MODEL / ATTRIBUTION_METHOD defaults are set.
  - Why override loses: on Brown the baseline's explicit tags are epithets ("the
    girl", "the doctor"), and these override the LLM's real names.
  - Remaining errors for the winner: ¶22 (the label above). In Brown the model
    says "Maggie" where gold has "Maggie MacNab"; the gold cast has no "Maggie"
    alias, so these count as wrong attributions although the voice heard is the
    expected one. ¶83 is one real Mrs MacNab → Maggie error.
  - Detection misses: 1 gold utterance in Alice, 0 in Brown.
  - Groq use: 14 candidate requests plus 7 verification requests and 7 for the
    listening runs.

  Prompt and rule changes

  - Prompt: written once, no revisions.
  - Baseline fixes (all candidates re-run after each one; LLM answers came from
    the cache):
    a. Lowercase noun phrases end before an -ly adverb or a preposition ("said
       the girl shortly" → the girl).
    b. they tags count as group speech, so they stay UNKNOWN and stop
       turn-taking. This fixed Alice ¶58.
    c. Sentence-initial pronouns and conjunctions are not names ("She said",
       "But I said"). These were taking voice slots in the whole-book ranking.
  - Cast: reports with the same name or an LLM alias always merge. Equal
    normalised names (Mr Smith / Mrs Smith), and subset or fuzzy matches, merge
    only when genders don't conflict and the LLM hadn't declared them separate
    characters.
  - Narration: a segment with no letters or digits is dropped before chunking. A
    lone ( in Alice ¶40 made edge-tts return no audio and failed the whole
    chapter on the first listening run.

  Spec interpretations

  - Turn-taking runs over paragraphs that contain a quote and have at most 20
    words of narration.
  - Pronoun tags look for named characters (names from explicit tags) mentioned
    in the current and previous 2 paragraphs; a character's gender comes from
    the pronouns in the 200 characters after its tags.
  - Distinct voices are capped at MAX_CAST in total, and the last voice of each
    pool is held back as the shared minor voice.
  - Attribution is scored once per gold utterance, using the predicted utterance
    of its first matched span.
  - "Same name" reading of "LLM declared distinct": a name the LLM adds as new,
    while a similar name is already in its list, is never fuzzy-merged with that
    name.
  - Retry wait limit: the 120 s limit is a constant in llm.py, not a config
    setting, because the phase prompt lists the new settings exactly.
  - Disk cache: used by the evaluator only. narrate.py doesn't use it.

  Things to know

  - Voices are fixed by the baseline ranking, so the voice map is weak. The
    ranking runs before narration, as D37 and §15.5 require. In the Alice book
    the March Hare and the Dormouse fall outside the top 8, so they get the
    shared minor voices, and the March Hare gets the female minor voice because
    the baseline couldn't tell its gender. In Brown, epithet names ("the
    priest", "the other") take distinct voices while Maggie ends up on the minor
    voice. This doesn't change the D39 result, but it is audible.
  - Possible SPEC edit: the new rule-4 exception isn't written into SPEC §15.2
    yet. I didn't edit the SPEC.

  Files

    - App: backend/app/dialogue/attribute.py, cast.py, llm.py, voices.py,
      windows.py
    - Tests: backend/tests/test_attribute.py, test_cast.py,

      tts/narrator.py
    - Scripts: scripts/narrate.py, scripts/evaluate_dialogue.py
    - Config, tests and docs: .env.example (LLM_MAX_REQUESTS_PER_MINUTE

      testset_dialogue/results.md

  - Output-token pacing (D42): LLM_OUTPUT_TOKENS_PER_MINUTE=1000 is a second
    rolling budget in the limiter, estimated as 12 × quote ids + 40 and
    corrected from usage. Tests use the fake clock.
  - Jobs and API:
    - narration_mode on upload, with 400 BAD_REQUEST for a bad value or for
      characters mode without a key;
    - status attributing, windows_total / windows_done, cast, per-chapter
      attribution, the fallback warning, and character_voices_available;
    - the worker attributes chapter N+1 as a task while chapter N is narrated,
      casts voices just before each chapter, cancels a pending attribution task
      on exit, and keeps single mode on the v1 path;
    - 5 API tests with a fake Groq, plus smoke_api.py --mode characters.
  - Frontend: the mode choice with the privacy and timing lines, "Finding the

      44 +  so.’`) are kept as one utterance.

      204  ## Limits and non-goals
      205  - Limits: `.pdf`/`.docx` only, 50 MB, 200 PDF pages, 100,000 w
           ords; one job at a time with up to 3
      206    waiting; 10 uploads per IP per hour; audio kept at most 24 h
            (a server restart also clears it).
      207 -- Not supported in v1: accounts or saved libraries, OCR / scan
          -ned PDFs, `.doc`/`.txt`/`.epub`,
      208 -  languages other than English, reading tables/captions/footno
          -tes/equations, multiple voices, paid APIs.
      207 +- Not supported: accounts or saved libraries, OCR / scanned PD
          +Fs, `.doc`/`.txt`/`.epub`,
      208 +  languages other than English, reading tables/captions/footno
          +tes/equations, paid APIs.
      209 +- Character voices: up to 8 distinct voices per book (others s
          +hare one minor voice per gender); a
      210 +  character keeps the first voice it gets, even if it turns ou
          +t more important later; speakers the
      211 +  text doesn't make clear are read by the narrator; 800 LLM re
          +quests per day, after which (or when
      212 +  Groq is down) the rule-based fallback is used and the book p
          +age says so.
      213 +- **Privacy:** in character-voices mode the document's text is
          + sent to Groq to find the speakers
      214 +  (stated on the upload page, D36). Single-narrator mode sends
          + text only to the TTS service.

● Update(docs\VIVA.md)
  ⎿  Added 18 lines
      35  - On a free Azure student VM it narrates 600–720 characters per
           second: *Alice in Wonderland*
      36    (2 h 36 min of audio) is ready in about 3 minutes, using abou
          t 130 MB of memory.
      37
      38 +### v2 in one minute: character voices
      39 +
      40 +**What.** An opt-in mode where each main character of a novel i
         +s read in their own voice and the
      41 +narrator reads the rest. Sample: `/sample/characters` (Alice, c
         +hapter 7: Alice, the Hatter, the
      42 +March Hare and the Dormouse each have a voice).
      43 +
      44 +**How.**
      45 +1. Find the dialogue: quote spans per paragraph, broken quotes
         +kept as one utterance.
      46 +2. Work out who speaks each utterance with an LLM (Groq, `qwen/
         +qwen3.8-27b`) on ~1,200-word
      47 +   windows, with a rule-based fallback (speech tags, pronouns,
         +turn-taking) whenever the LLM fails.
      48 +3. Merge character names into a cast and give voices lazily, ch
         +apter by chapter (D41).
      49 +4. Narrate with one voice per chunk and a 150 ms pause between
         +speakers.
      50 +
      51 +**Results.** Voice accuracy (the share of lines read in the rig
         +ht voice): **0.99 on the dev chapters,
      52 +0.83 on the held-out chapter (first and only run)**; the rule-b
         +ased baseline gets 0.71 / 0.48. Quote
      53 +detection F1 is 0.99 on both. The model and method were chosen
         +by a rule written down before any
      54 +results (D39).
      55 +

      58  ## 2. Where is the AI / data science?

● Update(docs\VIVA.md)
  ⎿  Added 16 lines
       83    - Those numbers caused real decisions: moving to Azure (D28)
           , edge-only narration on the server
       84      (D26), and a faster assembly step.
       85
       86 +### Where is the AI in v2?
       87 +- **LLM speaker attribution vs a rule baseline.** The same tas
          +k is solved two ways: hand-written
       88 +  rules (speech tags like `said Alice`, pronoun tags resolved
          +to the one named character of that
       89 +  gender, turn-taking in back-and-forth dialogue) and an LLM t
          +hat reads each window with numbered
       90 +  quotes (`[Q3]‘…’`) and returns JSON. The rules are also the
          +fallback, so the system degrades
       91 +  instead of failing.
       92 +- **A measured comparison of two LLMs.** qwen3.8-27b and gpt-o
          +ss-120b, each used alone and with
       93 +  "baseline override" (an explicit tag beats the LLM): five ca
          +ndidates, scored on the same labelled
       94 +  chapters, with tokens and latency recorded for every request
          +.
       95 +- **Evaluation design.** Hand-labelled chapters (a labelling t
          +ool and a label lint for consistency),
       96 +  three metrics (quote detection F1, attribution accuracy spli
          +t explicit/implicit, and voice accuracy
       97 +  as heard), a selection rule fixed before any results, and di
          +sk-cached LLM answers so re-scoring is
       98 +  free and repeatable.
       99 +- **Held-out honesty.** One chapter from a different book was
          +never opened; it was evaluated once,
      100 +  after everything was frozen, and that number (0.83) is what
          +I report, not the dev 0.99.

      200
      201  14. **What does v2 add?**
      202      - One voice per character: detect dialogue, use a free LLM
            to attribute each line to a speaker,
      203 -      merge aliases, and give each character a distinct voice.
      204 -    - The data model already has a `speaker` field on every se
          -gment for this (D11).
      205 -    - Attribution accuracy would be measured on a labelled cha
          -pter.
      203 +      merge aliases, and give each character a distinct voice.
          + It is opt-in; single narrator is
      204 +      unchanged.
      205 +    - The data model already had a `speaker` field on every se
          +gment for this (D11), so v1 needed no
      206 +      data-model change.
      207 +    - Attribution is measured on labelled chapters: 0.99 voice
          + accuracy on dev, 0.83 held-out.

      209  15. **How do you know where a chapter starts in a PDF without

      210      - Body size = the most common font size by character count

● Update(docs\VIVA.md)
  ⎿  Added 79 lines
      243        hyphenated form appears elsewhere in the document.
      244      - Render's proxy dropping keep-alive connections, which le
           d to retrying GETs in the client.
      245
      246 +### v2 questions
      247 +
      248 +21. **Why does the LLM beat the rules so clearly on "The Absen
          +ce of Mr Glass" (0.99 vs 0.38)?**
      249 +    - Chesterton tags speakers with descriptions, not names: "
          +said the priest", "the doctor
      250 +      answered", "said the girl shortly". The rules see an exp
          +licit tag but can't know that "the
      251 +      priest" is Father Brown, so the line gets its own wrong
          +voice.
      252 +    - The LLM reads the context and maps epithets to character
          +s. In *Alice* the tags are mostly
      253 +      names ("said the Hatter"), so the rules already reach 0.
          +91 there.
      254 +
      255 +22. **Why did "LLM + baseline override" make things worse?**
      256 +    - The idea was that an explicit `said X` tag should beat t
          +he LLM. But "explicit" to the rules
      257 +      includes epithets, so the override replaced the LLM's co
          +rrect "Father Brown" with "the priest".
      258 +    - Dev: override·qwen 0.87 vs llm·qwen 0.99; on the held-ou
          +t chapter 0.67 vs 0.83. Attribution
      259 +      accuracy stayed similar; the loss is in voices, because
          +epithets become separate characters.
      260 +
      261 +23. **Why is voice accuracy the selection metric, not attribut
          +ion accuracy?**
      262 +    - The listener hears voices, not names. Two different mino
          +r characters sharing the minor voice,
      263 +      or an unknown line read by the narrator, sound right eve
          +n when the name differs, and a correct
      264 +      name with the wrong voice sounds wrong.
      265 +    - It also measures the whole system (attribution, cast mer
          +ging and casting together).
      266 +
      267 +24. **How did Groq's rate limits shape the design?**
      268 +    - The free tier allows 8,000 tokens and, for this model, *
          +*1,000 completion tokens per minute**:
      269 +      about one window per minute. A 100,000-word book needs 3
          +0–60 minutes of attribution.
      270 +    - So: attribution runs one chapter ahead of narration (D37
          +), listening starts after chapter 1,
      271 +      the client paces both token budgets instead of hitting 4
          +29s (D42), responses are cached per
      272 +      job, and there is a daily cap of 800 requests.
      273 +
      274 +25. **What happens when Groq is down or out of budget?**
      275 +    - The affected windows use the rule-based baseline; the jo
          +b never fails because of the LLM
      276 +      (D34). The chapter is marked `baseline` or `mixed`, the
          +book page notes it, and a warning says
      277 +      how many chapters used the simpler method.
      278 +    - Tested with a fake Groq returning HTTP 500: every chapte
          +r becomes `baseline`, the job finishes.
      279 +
      280 +26. **Why is the grouping score below the 0.9 target on Alice,
          + and why doesn't it matter much?**
      281 +    - Grouping checks that the pieces of one utterance (a brok
          +en quote) are grouped the same way as in
      282 +      the labels. On Alice it is 0.79 (dev micro 0.90).
      283 +    - Most of the mismatches are broken quotes by *one* speake
          +r (`‘I do,’ Alice hastily replied;
      284 +      ‘at least…’`) that the labels split and the segmenter jo
          +ins. Same speaker, so the voice is the
      285 +      same. What would matter is joining two *different* speak
          +ers; that happens in 1 of 43 Alice
      286 +      multi-span utterances (2%), and I fixed the one real cas
          +e with a general rule.
      287 +
      288 +27. **How did you keep the labels consistent?**
      289 +    - Written conventions (D38): thoughts belong to the thinke
          +r, group speech is UNKNOWN, quoted
      290 +      writing is not an utterance, spans include the quote mar
          +ks.
      291 +    - A label lint checks the files without the segmenter: ove
          +rlapping spans, spans that don't start
      292 +      or end at a quote mark, spans that may contain narration
          +, and quotes dropped when a span was
      293 +      narrowed. It found real labelling errors before evaluati
          +on.
      294 +
      295 +28. **Why two LLMs, and why these two?**
      296 +    - They were the two capable models on the free Groq key (D
          +32). qwen3.8-27b won on voice accuracy
      297 +      and used fewer tokens and half the latency; gpt-oss-120b
          + has to "think" (at least at `low`
      298 +      effort), which cost 519 of its 895 completion tokens on
          +one window.
      299 +
      300 +29. **Did you check that "thinking off" really works?**
      301 +    - Yes, with real requests rather than trusting docs: qwen
          +gave 652 completion tokens with
      302 +      thinking on, off or unset, and never returned reasoning
          +text, so on Groq it doesn't think at all.
      303 +      gpt-oss rejects `none`; at `low` it reasons.
      304 +
      305 +30. **Why is the held-out score so much lower (0.83 vs 0.99)?*
          +*
      306 +    - The dev chapters were looked at while improving rules an
          +d fixing labels; the held-out chapter
      307 +      never was, so its number is the honest one.
      308 +    - The drop is in implicit lines (no speech tag naming the
          +speaker): 0.67 held-out. Explicit lines
      309 +      stay at 0.96. Long back-and-forth dialogue without tags
          +is where the model still guesses.
      310 +
      311 +31. **How are voices assigned, and why lazily?**
      312 +    - The first version gave voices up front from a rule-based
          + ranking of the whole book. Listening
      313 +      showed the problem: epithets took voice slots and the Ma
          +rch Hare got a female voice because the
      314 +      rules couldn't tell its gender.
      315 +    - Now (D41) the rule pass only reserves voices for clearly
          + named, frequent characters; everyone
      316 +      else gets a voice just before the first chapter they spe
          +ak in, with the LLM's gender, and keeps
      317 +      it. At most 8 distinct voices; one-line characters share
          + the minor voice.
      318 +
      319 +32. **Privacy in character-voices mode?**
      320 +    - The document's text is sent to Groq to find the speakers
          +. The upload page says so next to the
      321 +      option (D36), and the option is off by default.
      322 +    - Groq's free tier states it doesn't train on the data; no
          +thing is stored by us beyond the
      323 +      temporary job folder.
      324 +
      325  ---

      327  ## 4. Known limitations (say these plainly)

● Update(docs\VIVA.md)
  ⎿  Added 15 lines
      336  - Scanned PDFs are rejected (no OCR).
      337  - Jobs are kept in memory: a server restart or redeploy loses
           running jobs and their audio.
      338
      339 +**v2 (character voices):**
      340 +- Implicit lines (no tag naming the speaker) are the weak spot
          +: 0.67 attribution on the held-out
      341 +  chapter.
      342 +- At most 8 distinct voices, given first come first served aft
          +er the reservations: in the whole
      343 +  *Alice* book the Duchess, Caterpillar, Dormouse and March Ha
          +re end up on the shared minor voices,
      344 +  and a character whose gender the LLM leaves unknown gets the
          + minor voice of the larger pool (so the
      345 +  March Hare sounds female in later chapters). A voice never c
          +hanges once used.
      346 +- Epithets ("the priest") in rule-based fallback windows becom
          +e separate characters.
      347 +- Slow on long books: ≈ 1 window per minute on the free tier (
          +30–60 min of attribution for 100,000
      348 +  words); later chapters can wait for their speakers.
      349 +- Depends on a free third-party service and sends the text to
          +it; when it is down, the rules take
      350 +  over with lower accuracy.
      351 +- Evaluated on three chapters only (two dev, one held-out): th
          +e numbers are indicative, not precise.
      352 +- Production memory during a characters job on the VM: _to be
          +filled in after the production check_.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The grep returned "0 matches" as exit code 1 and stopped the chain, so the
  files are clean. Committing again:

  Pushed to main (ctrl+o to expand)

● Steps 1–7 are done and pushed (b45c704, 1e44ab9, 05d0df6). The held-out
  chapter ran once and gave voice accuracy 0.83 for the chosen setup (llm with
  qwen/qwen3.8-27b), against 0.99 on dev. I've stopped at step 8 and am waiting
  for your production check.

  What happened in each step

  1. Casting fix (D41).
  - The whole-book rule pass now only reserves voices; each chapter's speakers
    get their voices just before it is narrated, using the LLM's genders. The
    tests cover all five cases you listed.
  - Old vs new voice accuracy is identical on the current labels (qwen 186/187,
    gpt-oss 180/187, baseline 132/187). The metric compares the voice heard with
    the voice the system gave the right character, so changing who gets which
    voice can't move it. The fix shows in listening instead: in Alice ch. 7 the
    March Hare now has its own male voice, not the female minor voice.
  - D39 still picks llm with qwen, so D40 is unchanged. The rise from 0.989 to
    0.995 since 6.2 comes from your label fixes.

  2. Output-token pacing (D42). Added as a second per-minute budget. The tests
  use the fake clock.

  3. Jobs and API.
  - narration_mode on upload, the new job fields, the fallback warning and
    character_voices_available are all in.
  - The worker attributes chapter N+1 while chapter N is narrated, cancels that
    work when the job ends, and single mode takes the old path.
  - 5 API tests with a fake Groq, plus smoke_api.py --mode characters.
  - Whole Alice book, local, real Groq:

  | Measure            | Result
     |
  |--------------------|--------------------------------------------------------
  ----|
  | Total time         | 12:21
     |
  | Attribution        | about 11.5 min, overlapping narration and setting its
  pace |
  | Chapter 1 playable | after 1:00
     |
  | Windows            | 27, none fell back to the rules
     |
  | 429 waits          | 4, of 4–12 s each, despite the pacing
     |
  | Peak memory        | 115 MB (single mode was 131 MB on the VM)

  Left open
  - Voice slots on long books: with 8 distinct voices, characters who first
    speak after the slots are used get the shared minor voices. In the whole of
    Alice that's the Duchess, Caterpillar, Dormouse and March Hare. The LLM gave
    the March Hare an unknown gender, so it gets the female minor voice. This
    is in the docs as a limitation and as future work.
  - Lines without a speech tag are the weak spot on unseen text (0.67 on the
    held-out chapter).
  - Slow on long books: about 30–60 minutes of attribution for a 100,000-word
    book on Groq's free tier, as documented under D42.
  - Grouping: dev grouping is 0.90 (Alice 0.79). The gap is almost all one
    speaker's broken quotes split in the labels, which doesn't change the voice
    heard.
